id="sidebarContent"><p class="sidebar_navTopLabel__KLlOw">Contents</p><ol><li><a class="sidebar_link__NRCVd sidebar_linkActive__gwV6j" rel="noopener" href="#article">Article</a></li><li><a class="sidebar_link__NRCVd" rel="noopener" href="#more-blogs">More blogs</a></li></ol></div><div class="sidebar_copyUrlWrapper__AaTG4  hidden lg:flex" style="border-bottom:none"><button type="button" class="sidebar_copyUrl__cRt0X">Copy URL</button></div><div class="sidebar_copyUrlWrapper__AaTG4 flex lg:hidden" style="border-bottom:none;border-top:none"><button type="button" class="sidebar_copyUrl__cRt0X">Copy URL</button></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><div class="hidden lg:block" style="margin-bottom:-0px"></div><div class="" id="article"><div class=""><div class="bg-white "><div class="rebrand-container py-0"><div class="default-grid"><div class="col-span-full lg:col-start-4 wysiwyg_module_root__TObB_"><div class="wysiwyg_module_content__8emWC"><p>Recently, the Zscaler ThreatLabZ team came across a new RAT called InnfiRAT, which is written in .NET and designed to perform specific tasks from an infected machine. This blog provides an analysis of this new RAT, including the way it communicates, all the tasks it performs, and the information it steals.<br/>   </p>    <h3><strong>Background</strong></h3>    <p>As with just about every piece of malware, InnfiRAT is designed to access and steal personal information on a user&#x27;s computer. Among other things, InnfiRAT is written to look for cryptocurrency wallet information, such as Bitcoin and Litecoin. InnfiRAT also grabs browser cookies to steal stored usernames and passwords, as well as session data. In addition, this RAT has ScreenShot functionality so it can grab information from open windows. For example, if the user is reading email, the malware takes a screenshot. It also checks for other applications running on the system, such as an active antivirus program.  </p>    <p>InnfiRAT sends the data it has collected to its command-and-control (C&amp;C) server and requests further instructions. The C&amp;C can also instruct the malware to download additional payloads onto the infected system.<br/>   </p>    <h3><strong>Technical analysis</strong></h3>    <p><strong>1)</strong> Before executing the main payload, the malware initially checks whether the file is executing from %AppData% directory or not with the name <em>NvidiaDriver.exe</em>. If not, then a web request is sent to <strong>“iplogger[.]com/1HEt47&quot;</strong> (possibly to check network connectivity).</p>    <p><strong>2)</strong> It records all the running processes in an array, then iterates through each process and checks whether any process is running with the name NvidiaDriver.exe. If so, the malware kills that process and waits for an exit.<br/>  <br/>   <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/1.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 1: Checks execution location, terminates process with name NvidiaDriver         <br/>   </p>    <p><strong>3)</strong> InnfiRAT copies itself as <em>%AppData%/NvidiaDriver.exe</em> and executes it from <em>%AppData%</em> before terminating the current process.</p>    <h6> <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/2.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/>             <br/>  Figure 2: The malware makes a copy of itself in %AppData% <br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>4)</strong> After confirming the path of file execution, it writes a Base64 encoded PE file in memory, which is later decoded in its actual format and is loaded after changing the entry point of the file. This is also a .NET executable and contains the actual functionality of the malware.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/3.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 3: Embedded PE file in encoded form</h6>    <h6><br/>   <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/4.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 4: Embedded PE file is decoded and executed</h6>    <h3><br/>  <strong>Analysis of embedded .NET executable</strong></h3>    <p>All the strings inside the file are encoded with a custom encoding scheme that utilizes the XOR operation.<br/>  <br/>  <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/5.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 5: Strings decoding logic<br/>   </p>    <p>As the execution of the malware starts, it checks for the presence of VM environment. It does so by checking the return value from the routine <em><strong>JкыnеюwPреюLLщzьhdкXoJxбюHхрйFWрDлнруG7574208083337</strong></em>. If the return value is equal to the first value, <strong>enum[0],</strong> defined in the enum shown below, then it continues the execution or else it terminates.</p>    <h6><br/>  <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/6.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/> <br/>  <br/>  Figure 6: User-defined enum structure<br/>   </h6>    <p>After performing the VM checks, the malware obtains the <strong>country</strong> and<strong> HWID </strong>information of the machine it is running on. To obtain the country information, it calls the routine <em>EjarVhXфf8752612307563884480()</em> [FetchNetworkInfo] and fetches the <strong>Country</strong> key value from the returned data in JSON format. Similarly, to obtain the <strong>HWID,</strong> it calls the routine <strong><em>ubобмдGogBлzWKrgrыaZucвлC33208440168()</em></strong><em>.</em></p>    <h3><br/>  <strong>Anti-VM checks</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Inside the <em>JкыnеюwPреюLLщzьhdкXoJxбюHхрйFWрDлнруG7574208083337() </em>[VMDetection] routine:</strong></p>    <p><strong>Note:</strong> All the enum values are referenced using <strong>enum[index]</strong> during analysis where the <strong>index starts from 0.</strong></p>    <p><strong>1.</strong> Performs WMIquery to obtain the following information:</p>    <p><strong>&quot;Manufacturer&quot;<br/>  &quot;Caption&quot;<br/>  &quot;Name&quot;<br/>  &quot;ProcessorId&quot;<br/>  &quot;NumberOfCores&quot;<br/>  &quot;NumberOfLogicalProcessors&quot;<br/>  &quot;L2CacheSize&quot;<br/>  &quot;L3CacheSize&quot;<br/>  &quot;SocketDesignation&quot;</strong></p>    <p>It then checks, one-by-one, if the <strong>manufacturer </strong>contains one of the below-mentioned strings and returns the value from the <strong>enum </strong>as specified:</p>    <p><strong>“VBoxVBoxVBox”</strong>                   <strong>returns </strong>enum[2]<br/>  <strong>“VMwareVMware” </strong>                 <strong>returns </strong>enum[1]<br/>  <strong>“Prl hyperv     </strong>                          <strong>returns </strong>enum[3]<br/>  <strong>“Microsoft Corporation” </strong>       <strong>returns </strong>enum[4]</p>    <p><br/>  <strong>2.</strong> WMIquery is performed again but this time to obtain the following information:</p>    <p><strong>&quot;DeviceID&quot;<br/>  &quot;MediaType&quot;<br/>  &quot;Model&quot;<br/>  &quot;PNPDeviceID&quot;<br/>  &quot;SerialNumber&quot;</strong></p>    <p>A check is performed if the PnpDeviceId contains one of the below strings and returns the value from the enum as specified:</p>    <p><strong>“VBOX_HARDDISK”       </strong>      <strong>returns </strong>enum[2]<br/>  <strong>“VEN_VMWARE” </strong>                 <strong>returns </strong>enum[1]</p>    <p>If none of the above conditions match, it <strong>returns </strong>enum[0].</p>    <p> </p>    <h3><strong>Machine network information</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Inside the <em>EjarVhXфf8752612307563884480()</em> [FetchNetworkInfo] routine:</strong></p>    <p>A web request is sent to the following URL https://ipinfo[.]io/json and the received data is returned from the function. The received data contains the following information:</p>    <p> <strong> &quot;ip&quot;<br/>    &quot;city&quot;<br/>    &quot;region&quot;<br/>    &quot;country&quot;<br/>    &quot;loc&quot;<br/>    &quot;postal&quot;<br/>    &quot;org&quot;</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/7.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 7: Web request being made</h6>    <p> </p>    <h3><strong>Network communication</strong><br/>   </h3>    <p><strong>Inside the <em>мMлFкCцеGPбiбqюK1559516831() </em>[CreateDuplexChannel] routine:</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT sets up a duplex channel with the name “IVictim” using DuplexChannelFactory <strong>tcp://62[.]210[.]142[.]219:17231/IVictim</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/8.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 8: Creating a duplex channel with C&amp;C server<br/>   </h6>    <p>After forming the duplex channel with the name IVictim, it uses the IVictim interface, which contains the following methods:</p>    <p><strong>“Subscribe”<br/>  “CompleteTask”<br/>  “GetDlls”<br/>  “AvailableTasks”</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/9.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 9: Available methods in the IVictim interface</h6>    <p><br/>  <strong>Inside the <em>SуkdVkцiшkUояUuчPуюяmмuty187968776() </em>[SubscribeVictim] routine:</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT calls the subscriber method from the IVictim interface with login = “innfiniti”<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/10.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 10: The subscribe method from the IVictim interface is invoked</h6>    <p><br/>  <strong>Inside the <em>хaxeYхсиghIжNпDмвQюwkуpкgимuбсфbnдбMвMC67210633684721828()</em> [GetAndExecuteSpecifiedTask] routine:</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT obtains the tasks inside a UserTask list by invoking AvailableTasks where UserTask has the following keys:</p>    <p><strong>“ID”<br/>  “Action”<br/>  “URL”<br/>  “FinalPoint”<br/>  “Current” <br/>  “Status”<br/>  “Country”<br/>  “RunSilent”<br/>  “Argument”</strong></p>    <p>It iterates through each task. On each iteration, it first checks for the country value received to be equal to “<strong>ALL</strong>” <em>OR</em>  the one present in the BasicInfoVictim class, which was obtained earlier <em>AND</em> the <strong>action </strong>to perform is &quot;<strong>DownAndEx</strong>&quot; and the <strong>URL </strong>value is available.<br/>      <br/>  If the above conditions match, then the CompleteTasks method is called with three arguments: </p>    <p><strong>“login”<br/>  “hwid”<br/>  “TaskID”</strong><br/>   </p>    <p>The RAT calls the routine <strong><em>rLPсаWFоWcTjzпTэBFWkъмзтшпD147152108377454681517643543()</em> [ExecuteFile]</strong> with three arguments to execute the file.<br/>  Arg1 = Path of the file to be executed [obtained from the URL]<br/>  Arg2 = Arguments to the file to be executed [obtained from Argument key of current UserTask element]<br/>  Arg3 = true/false [Obtained from RunSilent key of current UserTask element]</p>    <p>After iterating all items in the UserTask list, it sleeps for 30,000 milliseconds.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/11.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 11: Country, action, and URL checks are performed and the specified task is completed<br/>   </h6>    <h3><strong>Process checks</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Inside the <em>LlсiсkнwychhVзjзNзxрFrUOE4656655235232302206601527615541285() </em>[ProcessCheck] routine:</strong></p>    <p>All the running processes in the system are obtained, their names are converted to lowercase and then a check is performed to see if the name matches with any of the following strings: </p>    <p><strong>“taskmgr”<br/>  “processhacker”<br/>  “procmon”<br/>  “procexp”<br/>  “pchunter”<br/>  “procexp64”</strong></p>    <p>If there are any matches, the process terminates. Below are the snapshots depicting the actions performed.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/12.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 12: Obtaining processes, converting their names to lowercase, checking specific processes</h6>    <p> </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/13.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 13: Converting ProcessName to lowercase</h6>    <p> </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/14.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 14: Checking for above-mentioned running processes (process names are obfuscated here)</h6>    <p><br/>  <strong>Inside wYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065() [KillProcesses] routine:</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT obtains the list of all processes running in the system and kills any process whose name contains one of the following strings:<br/>  <strong>“chrome”<br/>  “browser”<br/>  “firefox”<br/>  “opera”<br/>  “amigo”<br/>  “kometa”<br/>  “torch”<br/>  “orbitum”</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/16.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 15: Kills processes that contain any of the above-mentioned strings</h6>    <h6> </h6>    <h3><strong>Scheduled execution</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Inside the <em>эйviMhйсuьZCпJфшcкLйшuв348374() </em>[ScheduleMalwareExecution] routine:</strong></p>    <p>The CMD (cmd.exe) command string is constructed and executed to schedule the malware execution. The command string looks like below: </p>    <p><strong>/C schtasks /create /tn WindowsUpdater /tr &quot;%AppData%NvidiaDriver.exe &quot; /st HH:mm  /du 9999:59 /sc daily /ri 1 /f</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/15.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 16: CMD command is constructed and executed<br/>   </h6>    <h3><strong>C&amp;C commands</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Here are some tasks performed by the malware based on the commands received from C&amp;C server:</strong></p>    <p><strong>1. SendUrlAndExecute(string URL)</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT downloads the file from the specified URL by calling the routine <strong><em>жRfаeQbrwйfsLGыhчUrEжьFхaяGчрлCдtGжSofьQvдnIмs8383484343838630833542717281211() </em>[DownloadFileFromUrl]</strong>. Inside this routine, a directory is first created with the name TEMP inside the %AppData% if it doesn’t exist. Then the file is downloaded and saved inside this folder with the name extracted from the passed URL. The URL passed is broken into parts via delimiter ‘/’ and the last item is used as the file name.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/17.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 17: Create folder and download file<br/>   </h6>    <p>Once the download is complete, it calls the routine <strong><em>rLPсаWFоWcTjzпTэBFWkъмзтшпD147152108377454681517643543()</em> [ExecuteFile]</strong> with three arguments to execute the downloaded file.<br/>  <br/>  Arg1 = Path of the file to be executed<br/>  Arg2 = Arguments to the file to be executed<br/>  Arg3 = true<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/18.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 18: Execute the downloaded file</h6>    <p><br/>  <strong>2. ProfileInfo()</strong></p>    <p>Inside the routine, it collects the following information:</p>    <p><strong>“NetworkInfo”:{<br/>  &quot;ip&quot; <br/>  &quot;city&quot;<br/>  &quot;region&quot;<br/>  &quot;country&quot;<br/>  &quot;loc&quot;<br/>  &quot;postal&quot;<br/>  &quot;org&quot;<br/>  }<br/>  “PCAdmin”<br/>  “PCInformation” :{<br/>  “FrameWorkDescription”<br/>  “Processors”<br/>  “PRocessorsCore”<br/>  “VideoCards”<br/>  } </strong></p>    <p>It then sends the information to the C&amp;C server.<br/>  <br/>  <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/19.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 19: UserProfile info being collected and sent to the C&amp;C server<br/>   </p>    <p><strong>3. LoadLogs()</strong></p>    <p>It calls the GetDlls() routine, which obtains information inside a list of type DownloadDll where DownloadDll has two keys:</p>    <p><strong>“Path”,                     represents a relative path to an .exe file<br/>  “ByteArray”            binary data</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/20.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 20: GetDlls being called<br/>   </h6>    <p>After fetching the list, InnfiRAT traverses each element inside the list via a for-loop. Inside the for-loop:</p>    <p>The value of the <strong>Path </strong>key is split using delimiter “\\”. The second value in the split is the name of the directory. A check is performed to see if the count after the split is greater than 2 and there is no directory with the name obtained from the Path key split inside the executing module directory. If the check is true, a directory with the obtained name is created. </p>    <p>A check is performed if no file exists specified by Path key in the executing module directory. If the check is true, it creates the file and writes the value of <strong>ByteArray </strong>to this created file. </p>    <p>The routine <strong><em>wYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065() </em>[KillProcesses]</strong> is called.</p>    <p>Finally, data obtained from <strong>UserProfile()</strong> is sent to the C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/21.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 21: A directory is created, file is created, and KillProcesses is called; response is sent to the C&amp;C server</h6>    <p> </p>    <p><strong>4. LoadCookies()  - Steal Browser Cookie information</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT calls the GetDlls() routine, which obtains information inside a list of type DownloadDll where DownloadDll has two keys:</p>    <p><strong>“Path”                    represents a relative path to an .exe file<br/>  “ByteArray”          binary data</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/22.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 22: GetDlls being called<br/>   </h6>    <p>After fetching the list, the malware traverses each element inside the list via for-loop. The following occurs inside the for-loop:</p>    <p>The value of the <strong>Path </strong>key is split using the delimiter “\\”. Second, the value in the split is the name of the directory. A check is performed if the count after the split is greater than 2 and there is no directory with the name obtained from the Path key split inside the executing module directory. If the check is true, a directory with the obtained name is created. </p>    <p>A check is performed if no file exists specified by the Path key in the executing module directory. If a check is true, it creates the file and writes the value of <strong>ByteArray </strong>to this created file. <br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/23.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 23: Directory is created, file is created<br/>   </h6>    <p>It creates an empty list of <strong>BrowserCook </strong>type where BrowserCook has two keys, namely:<br/>  <strong>“CookiePaths”<br/>  “BrowserName”</strong></p>    <p>The name and corresponding cookie path are retrieved for the following browsers one by one:</p>    <p><strong>“Chrome”<br/>  “Yandex”<br/>  “Kometa”<br/>  “Amigo”<br/>  “Torch”<br/>  “Orbitum”<br/>  “Opera”<br/>  “Mozilla”</strong></p>    <p>A BrowserCook type element is created with the fetched information and is added to the list created earlier.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/24.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 24: Browser info is retrieved and added to the list<br/>   </h6>    <p>It creates an empty list of BrowserCookie type where <strong>BrowserCookie </strong>has three keys, namely: <br/>  <strong>“Browser”<br/>  “FileName”<br/>  “FileArray”</strong></p>    <p>Inside, two for-loop elements of the BrowserCookie type are created, where the Browser key and FileArray key are both assigned values using the information from the previously created BrowserCook list and the <strong>FileName </strong>is set to <strong>_Cookie.txt</strong> if the browser name for the current element is not <strong>“Mozilla”,</strong> or else it is set to<strong> Cookie.txt.</strong><br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/25.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 25: BrowserCookie elements list is built<br/>   </h6>    <p>The harvested BrowserCookie list is then sent to the C&amp;C server and the temporary file and directory are deleted.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/26.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 26: File and directory is deleted</h6>    <p><br/>  <strong>5. LoadWallets() - Steal Bitcoin Wallets</strong></p>    <p>The malware creates an empty list of the BitcoinWallet type where BitcoinWallet has two keys, namely:<br/>  <strong>“WalletArray”<br/>  “WalletName”</strong></p>    <p>A check is performed to see if a file for a Litecoin or Bitcoin wallet is present in the system at the following location:</p>    <p><strong>Litecoin:</strong> %AppData%\Litecoin\wallet.dat<br/>  <strong>Bitcoin: </strong>%AppData%\Bitcoin\wallet.dat</p>    <p>If it is found, then the element of type BitcoinWallet is added to the list after assigning a name to the <strong>WalletName </strong>key and reading the corresponding wallet file in the <strong>WalletArray </strong>key.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/27.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 27: File presence is checked, BitcoinWallet element is added to the list<br/>   </h6>    <p>Finally, the created list is sent in response to the C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/28.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 28: List is sent in response to the C&amp;C server<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>6. LoadFiles() - Steal small text files potentially containing sensitive information</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT collects all the .txt files available on the desktop whose size is less than 2,097,152 bytes inside a list of CustomFile types. <strong>CustomFile </strong>has two keys namely: <br/>  <strong>“Name” </strong><br/>  <strong> “FileArray”</strong></p>    <p>The created list is sent in response to the C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/29.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 29: Files are collected and sent to the C&amp;C server<br/>   </h6>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/30.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 30: Inside <em>HcапkцтеuxчI46156665847187238336657104255061.лQtdjюAKMCdскHUжfъqZTzmMнуз68532317728035381607276587242500</em> [CollectFiles]<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>7. LoadProcesses() - Get the list of running processes on the victim machine</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT creates an empty list of type <strong>ProcessInfo </strong>where ProcessInfo has three keys, namely:<br/>  <strong>“ID”<br/>  “Name” </strong><br/>  <strong>“Path”</strong></p>    <p>It obtains the list of all the processes running in the system and sends the list in response to the C&amp;C server. <br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/31.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 31: Process information is obtained and the list is sent to the C&amp;C server<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>8. Kill(int process) - Command to Kill a specific process on the victim machine</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT obtains the list of all the processes running in the system and then inside a for-loop, the processID of obtained processes is compared with the processID passed as an argument to this routine one at a time. If there is a match, the process is killed and the flag variable is set to true.</p>    <p>Finally, a response is sent to C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/32.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 32: Process is killed and response is sent<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>9. Screenshot() - Take a screenshot on the victim machine</strong></p>    <p>It calls the <strong><em>qюFpьGoJv97921676245()</em> [CaptureScreenshot] </strong>routine and the returned value is sent to the C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/33.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 33: Screenshot captured and sent to the C&amp;C server<br/>   </h6>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/34.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 34: Inside the qюFpьGoJv97921676245() [CaptureScreenshot] routine<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>10. RunCommand(string command) - Execute specified command on the victim machine</strong></p>    <p>This creates a new CMD process, builds the command line argument using the command passed as an argument to this routine, and finally starts the process.</p>    <p><strong>Command line argument:</strong>   /c  +  “ ” + command<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/35.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 35: Received command is executed<br/>   </h6>    <p><strong>11. ClearCooks() - Clears browser Cookies on the victim machine for specific Browsers</strong></p>    <p>InnfiRAT creates an empty list of <strong>BrowserCook </strong>type where BrowserCook has two keys, namely:<br/>  <strong>“CookiePaths” </strong><br/>  <strong>“BrowserName”</strong></p>    <p>The name and corresponding cookie path are retrieved for the following browsers one by one:</p>    <p><strong>“Chrome”<br/>  “Yandex”<br/>  “Kometa”<br/>  “Amigo”<br/>  “Torch”<br/>  “Orbitum”<br/>  “Opera”<br/>  “Mozilla”</strong><br/>   </p>    <p>A BrowserCook type element is created with the fetched information and is added to the list created earlier.<br/>  <br/>  <br/>  <img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/36.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 36: Browser info is retrieved and added to the list<br/>   </p>    <p>The routine <strong><em>wYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065()</em> [KillProcesses]</strong> is called.</p>    <p>The BrowserCook type list created earlier is traversed and cookies files are deleted using CookiePaths key value.</p>    <p>Finally, a response is sent to the C&amp;C server.<br/>   </p>    <h6><img alt="Image" src="https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/37.png" loading="lazy" decoding="async"/><br/>  <br/>  Figure 37: The routine wYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065() [KillProcesses] is called, cookie files are deleted, and response is sent to the C&amp;C server</h6>    <h3><strong>Conclusion</strong></h3>    <p>A RAT, remote-access trojan, is a type of malware that includes a backdoor, giving intruders the ability to control the targeted computer remotely and enabling them to perform any number of tasks, such as logging keystrokes, accessing confidential information, activating the system&#x27;s webcam, taking screenshots, formatting drives, and more. They can also be designed to spread to other systems on a network.</p>    <p>Because RATs are usually downloaded as a result of a user opening an email attachment or downloading an application that has been infected, the first line of defense is often the users who must, as always, refrain from downloading programs or opening attachments that aren&#x27;t from a trusted source.</p>    <p>The ThreatLabZ team continues to monitor this threat and ensure that Zscaler customers are protected.</p>    <p> </p>    <h3><strong>IOCs</strong></h3>    <p><strong>Md5:</strong> f992dd6dbe1e065dff73a20e3d7b1eef</p>    <p><strong>Downloading URL:</strong> rgho[.]st/download/6yghkhzgm/84986b88fe9d7e3caf5183e4342e713adf6c3040/df3049723db33889ac49202cb3a2f21ac1b82d5b/peugeot.zip</p>    <p><strong>NetworkURL:</strong> tcp://62[.]210[.]142[.]219:17231/IVictim</p>

the Zscaler ThreatLabZ team came across a new RAT called InnfiRAT, which is written in .NET and designed to perform specific tasks from an infected machine. This blog provides an analysis of this new RAT, including the way it communicates, all the tasks it performs, and the information it steals.\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBackground\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e    \u003cp\u003eAs with just about every piece of malware, InnfiRAT is designed to access and steal personal information on a user's computer. Among other things, InnfiRAT is written to look for cryptocurrency wallet information, such as Bitcoin and Litecoin. InnfiRAT also grabs browser cookies to steal stored usernames and passwords, as well as session data. In addition, this RAT has ScreenShot functionality so it can grab information from open windows. For example, if the user is reading email, the malware takes a screenshot. It also checks for other applications running on the system, such as an active antivirus program.  \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eInnfiRAT sends the data it has collected to its command-and-control (C\u0026amp;C) server and requests further instructions. The C\u0026amp;C can also instruct the malware to download additional payloads onto the infected system.\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTechnical analysis\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e1)\u003c/strong\u003e Before executing the main payload, the malware initially checks whether the file is executing from %AppData% directory or not with the name \u003cem\u003eNvidiaDriver.exe\u003c/em\u003e. If not, then a web request is sent to \u003cstrong\u003e“iplogger[.]com/1HEt47\"\u003c/strong\u003e (possibly to check network connectivity).\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e2)\u003c/strong\u003e It records all the running processes in an array, then iterates through each process and checks whether any process is running with the name NvidiaDriver.exe. If so, the malware kills that process and waits for an exit.\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e   \u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/1.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 1: Checks execution location, terminates process with name NvidiaDriver         \u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e3)\u003c/strong\u003e InnfiRAT copies itself as \u003cem\u003e%AppData%/NvidiaDriver.exe\u003c/em\u003e and executes it from \u003cem\u003e%AppData%\u003c/em\u003e before terminating the current process.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e \u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/2.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e             \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 2: The malware makes a copy of itself in %AppData% \u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e4)\u003c/strong\u003e After confirming the path of file execution, it writes a Base64 encoded PE file in memory, which is later decoded in its actual format and is loaded after changing the entry point of the file. This is also a .NET executable and contains the actual functionality of the malware.\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/3.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 3: Embedded PE file in encoded form\u003c/h6\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cbr\u003e   \u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/4.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 4: Embedded PE file is decoded and executed\u003c/h6\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cstrong\u003eAnalysis of embedded .NET executable\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e    \u003cp\u003eAll the strings inside the file are encoded with a custom encoding scheme that utilizes the XOR operation.\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  \u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/5.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 5: Strings decoding logic\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eAs the execution of the malware starts, it checks for the presence of VM environment. It does so by checking the return value from the routine \u003cem\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eJкыnеюwPреюLLщzьhdкXoJxбюHхрйFWрDлнруG7574208083337\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/em\u003e. If the return value is equal to the first value, \u003cstrong\u003eenum[0],\u003c/strong\u003e defined in the enum shown below, then it continues the execution or else it terminates.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/6.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e \u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 6: User-defined enum structure\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003eAfter performing the VM checks, the malware obtains the \u003cstrong\u003ecountry\u003c/strong\u003e and\u003cstrong\u003e HWID \u003c/strong\u003einformation of the machine it is running on. To obtain the country information, it calls the routine \u003cem\u003eEjarVhXфf8752612307563884480()\u003c/em\u003e [FetchNetworkInfo] and fetches the \u003cstrong\u003eCountry\u003c/strong\u003e key value from the returned data in JSON format. using \u003cstrong\u003eenum[index]\u003c/strong\u003e during analysis where the \u003cstrong\u003eindex starts from 0.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e1.\u003c/strong\u003e Performs WMIquery to obtain the following information:\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e\"Manufacturer\"\u003cbr\u003e \u003cstrong\u003e“Microsoft Corporation” \u003c/strong\u003e       \u003cstrong\u003ereturns \u003c/strong\u003eenum[4]\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cstrong\u003e2.\u003c/strong\u003e WMIquery is performed again but this time to obtain the following information:\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e\"DeviceID\"\u003cbr\u003e \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInside the \u003cem\u003eэйviMhйсuьZCпJфшcкLйшuв348374() \u003c/em\u003e[ScheduleMalwareExecution] routine:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eThe CMD The command string looks like below: \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e/C schtasks /create /tn WindowsUpdater /tr \"%AppData%NvidiaDriver.exe \" /st HH:mm  /du 9999:59 /sc daily /ri 1 /f\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/15.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 16: CMD command is constructed and executed\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/h6\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eC\u0026amp;C commands\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHere are some tasks performed by the malware based on the commands received from C\u0026amp;C server:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e1. SendUrlAndExecute(string URL)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eInnfiRAT downloads the file from the specified URL by calling the routine \u003cstrong\u003e\u003cem\u003eжRfаeQbrwйfsLGыhчUrEжьFхaяGчрлCдtGжSofьQvдnIмs8383484343838630833542717281211() \u003c/em\u003e[DownloadFileFromUrl]\u003c/strong\u003e. Inside this routine, a directory is first created with the name TEMP inside the %AppData% if it doesn’t exist. Then the file is downloaded and saved inside this folder with the name extracted from the passed URL. The URL passed is broken into parts via delimiter ‘/’ and the last item is used as the file name.\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/17.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 17: Create folder and download file\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003eOnce the download is complete, it calls the routine \u003cstrong\u003e\u003cem\u003erLPсаWFоWcTjzпTэBFWkъмзтшпD147152108377454681517643543()\u003c/em\u003e [ExecuteFile]\u003c/strong\u003e with three arguments to execute the downloaded file.\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Arg1 = Path of the file to be executed\u003cbr\u003e  Arg2 = Arguments to the file to be executed\u003cbr\u003e  Arg3 = true\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/18.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 18: Execute the downloaded file\u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cstrong\u003e2. Inside the for-loop:\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eThe value of the \u003cstrong\u003ePath \u003c/strong\u003ekey is split using delimiter “\\\\”. The second value in the split is the name of the directory. A check is performed to see if the count after the split is greater than 2 and there is no directory with the name obtained from the Path key split inside the executing module directory. If the check is true, a directory with the obtained name is created. \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eA check is performed if no file exists specified by Path key in the executing module directory. If the check is true, it creates the file and writes the value of \u003cstrong\u003eByteArray \u003c/strong\u003eto this created file. \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eThe routine \u003cstrong\u003e\u003cem\u003ewYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065() \u003c/em\u003e[KillProcesses]\u003c/strong\u003e is called.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eFinally, data obtained from \u003cstrong\u003eUserProfile()\u003c/strong\u003e is sent to the C\u0026amp;C server.\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/21.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 21: A directory is created, file is created, and KillProcesses is called; response is sent to the C\u0026amp;C server\u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e4. LoadCookies()  - Steal Browser Cookie information\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eInnfiRAT calls the GetDlls() routine, which obtains information inside a list of type DownloadDll where DownloadDll has two keys:\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e“Path”                    represents a relative path to an .exe file\u003cbr\u003e  “ByteArray”          binary data\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch6\u003e\u003cimg alt=\"Image\" src=\"https://cms.zscaler.com/cdn-cgi/image/format=auto/sites/default/files/images/blogs/Costura_RAT/22.png\" loading=\"lazy\" decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 22: GetDlls being called\u003cbr\u003e   \u003c/h6\u003e    \u003cp\u003eAfter fetching the list, the malware traverses each element inside the list via for-loop. The following occurs inside the for-loop:\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eThe value of the \u003cstrong\u003ePath \u003c/strong\u003ekey is split using the delimiter “\\\\”. decoding=\"async\"\u003e\u003cbr\u003e  \u003cbr\u003e  Figure 37: The routine wYxйыrоyTHuLдTч212065() [KillProcesses] is called, cookie files are deleted, and response is sent to the C\u0026amp;C server\u003c/h6\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eConclusion\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e    \u003cp\u003eA RAT, remote-access trojan, is a type of malware that includes a backdoor, giving intruders the ability to control the targeted computer remotely and enabling them to perform any number of tasks, such as logging keystrokes, accessing confidential information, activating the system's webcam, taking screenshots, formatting drives, and more. They can also be designed to spread to other systems on a network.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eBecause RATs are usually downloaded as a result of a user opening an email attachment or downloading an application that has been infected, the first line of defense is often the users who must, as always, refrain from downloading programs or opening attachments that aren't from a trusted source.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003eThe ThreatLabZ team continues to monitor this threat and ensure that Zscaler customers are protected.\u003c/p\u003e    \u003cp\u003e \u003c/p\u003e    \u003ch3\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIOCs\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e
